## AI Research

### [LLMs as Giant Lookup-Tables of Shallow Circuits](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/a9KqqgjN8gc3Mzzkh/llms-as-giant-lookup-tables-of-shallow-circuits)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>Early 2026 LLMs in scaffolds, from simple ones such as giving the model access to a scratchpad/"<a href="https://blog.research.google/2022/05/language-models-perform-reasoning-via.html">chain of thought</a>" up to <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Model_Context_Protocol">MCP servers</a>,
<a href="https://claude.com/skills">skills</a>, and <a href="https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/compaction">context compaction</a> &amp;c are <em>quite</em> capable. (<a href="https://metr.org/time-horizons/">Obligatory meme link to the METR graph</a>.)</p>
<p>Yet: If someone had told me in 2019 that systems with such capability would exist
in 2026, I would strongly predict that they would be almost uncontrollable
optimizers, ruthlessly &amp; tirelessly pursuing their goals and finding <a href="https://arbital.com/p/edge_instantiation/">edge
instantiations</a> in everything.
But they don't seem to be doing that. Current-day LLMs are just not <em>that</em>
optimizer-y, they appear to have capable behavior without apparent <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/osxNg6yBCJ4ur9hpi/does-agent-like-behavior-imply-agent-like-architecture">agent
structure</a>.</p>
<p>Discussions from the time either ruled out giant lookup-tables (<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/oxsBpx9v3bgxraiPj/towards-a-formalization-of-the-agent-structure-problem">Altair 2024</a>):</p>
<blockquote>
<p>One obvious problem is that there could be a policy which is the
equivalent of a giant look-up table it's just a list of key-value pairs
where the previous observation sequence is the look-up key, and it returns
a next action. For any well-performing policy, there could exist a table
version of it. These are clearly not of interest, and in some sense they
have no "structure" at all, let alone agent structure. A way to filter
out the look-up tables is to put an upper bound on the description length
of the policies […].</p>
</blockquote>
<p>or specified that the optimizer must be in the causal
history of such a giant lookup-table (<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/osxNg6yBCJ4ur9hpi/does-agent-like-behavior-imply-agent-like-architecture">Garrabrant
2019</a>):</p>
<blockquote>
<p>First a giant look-up table is not (directly) a counterexample. This
is because it might be that the only way to produce an agent-like GLUT
is to use agent-like architecture to search for it. Similarly a program
that outputs all possible GLUTs is also not a counterexample because you
might have to use your agent-like architecture to point at the specific
counterexample. A longer version of the conjecture is “If you see a
program impelements agent-like behavior, there must be some agent-like
architecture in the program itself, in the causal history of the program,
or in the process that brought your attention to the program.”</p>
</blockquote>
<p>The most fitting rejoinder to the observation
of capable non-optimizer AIs is probably <a href="https://www.allmusicals.com/lyrics/hamilton/alexanderhamilton.htm"><em>"Just you
wait"</em></a>—current
LLMs are <em>capable</em>, sure, but they're not wildly superhuman
to an extent comparable to the original worries about extreme
optimization pressure. In this view, they're molting into
full agency right now, and we should see the problems of high
optimization pressure show up by the end of 2026 or the five years
after<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-4ZKjyFojJHza244qn-1">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-4ZKjyFojJHza244qn-1">[1]</a></sup>
    </span> if they're not hidden from us by <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/r9tYkB2a8Fp4DN8yB/p/zthDPAjh9w6Ytbeks">deceptive
AIs</a>.
Indeed, current LLMs <a href="https://metr.org/blog/2025-06-05-recent-reward-hacking/"><em>do</em>
reward-hack</a>,
though the developers have been decent at suppressing the tendency down
to a consumer-acceptable level.</p>
<p>But I have a different theory for how LLMs can be capable without being
agentic/perciniously optimizing:</p>
<p><strong>LLMs are superlinear-in-network-width
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lookup_table">lookup-table</a>-like
collections of <em>depth-limited</em>, <em>composeable</em> and
<em><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nWRj6Ey8e5siAEXbK/simple-versus-short-higher-order-degeneracy-and-error-1">error-correcting</a></em>
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circuit_(computer_theory)">circuits</a>,
<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/comp-in-sup">computed in superposition</a></strong>.</p>
<p>One could call this the GLUT-of-circuits model of LLMs.</p>
<p>To elaborate:</p>
<ol>
<li>"<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lookup_table">lookup-table</a>-like": A common issue in <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/oxsBpx9v3bgxraiPj/towards-a-formalization-of-the-agent-structure-problem">proving theorems about agent structure</a> is to avoid including giant lookup tables where each sequence of previous percepts and actions is matched to an optimal next action. Such a giant lookup table is infeasible in the real world, but I think something <em>similar</em> to a giant lookup table might be possible, namely through computation in superposition.</li>
<li>"circuits": Turing machines aren't a great model for the type of computation that neural networks use (namely because they assume an infinite tape and arbitrary serial depth); a slightly better one is <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circuit_(computer_science)">circuits</a>, which have limited depth, though in computer science circuits are usually defined for booleans or integers.</li>
<li>"computed in superposition": Current LLMs <a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2022/toy_model/index.html">represent features in superposition</a>, that is, they exploit the fact that high-dimensional spaces can have exponentially many almost-orthogonal vectors relative to the number of dimensions (a consequence of the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johnson-Lindenstrauss_lemma">Johnson-Lindenstrauss lemma</a>). The idea of <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/comp-in-sup">computation in superposition</a> generalizes this observation to cram more computation into a neural network.</li>
<li>"superlinear-in-network-width": Computation in superposition allows for running many circuits at the same time. E.g. <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.05451">Hänni et al. 2024</a> construct (shallow) neural networks of witdh  that are able emulate an -sparse<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-4ZKjyFojJHza244qn-2">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-4ZKjyFojJHza244qn-2">[2]</a></sup>
    </span> boolean circuit of width  (<strong>Theorem 8</strong>).
<ol>
<li>This result gestures at the possibility that one <em>can</em> put a superlinear number of circuits<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-4ZKjyFojJHza244qn-3">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-4ZKjyFojJHza244qn-3">[3]</a></sup>
    </span> into a single neural network, making the neural network more of a lookup-table than an example of <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/6mysMAqvo9giHC4iX/what-s-general-purpose-search-and-why-might-we-expect-to-see">general-purpose search</a>.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>"depth-limited": A forward pass in SOTA language models has a limited number of steps of <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serial_computation">serial computation</a>; <a href="https://niplav.site/doc/cs/ai/language_models_are_few_shot_learners_brown_et_al_2020.pdf">gpt-3-davinci</a> had 96 layers, which results in a circuit depth of 78-83 steps per layer, impliying in  serial steps per forward pass. Current LLMs might look different due to sparse attention and other developments in architecture, no to speak of increased model size, but I'd guess that current frontier models don't have a circuit depth of more than 20000, unless I really messed something up in my calculation. Claude Sonnet 4.5 estimates that Kimi K2.5 Thinking has slightly less than 5000 serial steps of computation, mostly due to being only 61 layers deep.</li>
<li>"composeable": If the neural network just contained an unrelated collection of circuits, the network wouldn't be effective at solving difficult math problems. Instead, my best guess is that the circuits are selected by reinforcement learning, especially RLVR, to be <em>composeable</em>, that is each circuit takes inputs of the same type as outputs of other circuits in the network. (Maybe this explains the some of the "sameness"/"slop"-factor of LLM outputs, the "semantic type" has to match?)</li>
<li>"error-correcting": If a forward pass executes many circuits in superposition, there will be some interference between individual circuits, so circuits will be selected to be either robust to small errors or error-correcting. This is oddly similar to results from singular learning theory, which imply that (very roughly) <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nWRj6Ey8e5siAEXbK/simple-versus-short-higher-order-degeneracy-and-error-1">Bayesian inference selects for error-correcting programs</a>. I don't know which implications this would have.</li>
</ol>
<p>Estimate on the circuit depth of gpt-3-davinci:</p>
<ol>
<li>Self-attention
<ol>
<li> projections: single step</li>
<li>Compute : <code>matmul</code> of square matrix with size 12288, at logarithmic circuit depth for <code>matmul</code>s we get 13-14 steps</li>
<li><code>softmax</code> over an array of length  has depth  (summation of a list can be done via a summation in a binary tree), for a vector size of 2048 we get 11 steps</li>
<li>Multiply by , one <code>matmul</code>, 13-14 steps</li>
<li>Output projection, another <code>matmul</code>, 13-14 steps</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Feed-forward
<ol>
<li>First linear layer: one <code>matmul</code>, 13-14 steps</li>
<li><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Activation_function">GELU</a>: single step</li>
<li>Second linear layer: another <code>matmul</code>, 13-14 steps</li>
</ol>
</li>
</ol>
<p>Inferences from this model:</p>
<p><strong>Circuit selection</strong>: This model would imply that circuits are
selected mostly by another algorithm with very small serial depth,
relying on features of a problem that can be determined by very parallel
computations.</p>
<p>That somewhat matches my observations from looking at LLMs trying to
tackle problems: It often looks to me like they try one strategy after
another, and less often use detailed information from the past failed
attempt to form a complicated new strategy.</p>
<p>It <em>also</em> matches what we've seen from LLMs
<a href="https://palisaderesearch.org/blog/shutdown-resistance">self-preserving</a>/<a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/agentic-misalignment">black-mailing</a>/reward-hacking:
The actions seem opportunistic, not carefully hidden once they've been
performed, not embedded in larger plans; they look mostly like "another
strategy to try, oops, I guess that didn't quite work".</p>
<p><strong>Alignment</strong>: My guess is that most or almost all of
these circuits are <em>individually aligned</em> through bog-standard
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reinforcement_learning_from_human_feedback">RLHF</a>/<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_AI">Constitutional
AI</a>.
This works because the standard problems of <a href="https://arbital.com/p/edge_instantiation/">edge
instantiation</a> and <a href="https://niplav.site/doc/cs/ai/alignment/agent_foundations/categorizing_variants_of_goodharts_law_manheim_garrabrant_2019.pdf">Goodhart's
law</a>
don't show up as strongly, because the optimization mainly occurs
by either:</p>
<ol>
<li>Selecting one circuit from the giant lookup table that is all the circuits in superposition in the LLM</li>
<li>Running many circuits in superposition and selecting the best result or aggregating the best results.</li>
</ol>
<p>In this model every circuit is individually "aligned" (insofar such a
shallow program can be misaligned at all). Chain of "thought" composes
calls to related circuits (though more on CoT below).</p>
<p>If this view is correct, a folk view of alignment as simply
"deleting/downweighting the bad parts of a model" would be <em>mostly
correct</em>: There would be a large but finite number of circuits embedded
in the model, which can be upweighted, downweighted or outright
deleted by gradient descent. My extremely speculative guess is that
there is less than a quadrillion circuits stored in superposition in
a trillion-parameter model, which thorough-enough safety
training could exhaustively or near-exhaustively check and select. In this view, AI alignment really <em>would</em> be purely bottlenecked on the amount of computation spent on whac-a-moling unaligned circuits.</p>
<p>People might not spend enough compute on alignment training, and that
would still be a problem (though a lesser one, since the model wouldn't be
actively working against the developers), but the problem of alignment
would've been turned from a <a href="https://musingsandroughdrafts.wordpress.com/2021/04/05/on-category-i-and-category-ii-problems/">category I problem into a category II
problem</a>.</p>
<p><strong>Chain of thought</strong>: The obvious wrinkle in this story is that I haven't
talked about chain-of-"thought"/"reasoning" LLMs. It goes with saying that
long chains of thought enable vastly more serial computation to be done,
and I haven't yet quite teased apart how this impacts the overall picture
(besides "it makes it worse").</p>
<p>Still, some guesses at implications from the GLUT-of-circuits models
for alignment and chain-of-"thought":</p>
<ol>
<li>The token bottleneck is <em>real</em>. Every &lt;10k serial steps the entire state needs to be compressed down to one of 50k-200k tokens, resulting in at most 16-20 bits of state to be passed between each circuit. My guess is that it's actually closer to ~8-10 bits (given <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entropy_(information)#Introduction">~1 bit per character in English</a> (though <a href="https://cs.stanford.edu/people/eroberts/courses/soco/projects/1999-00/information-theory/entropy_of_english_9.html">this source</a> claims almost five bits per character!)), so maybe 2 bits per character in an optimized chain of "thought", at four to five characters per token. A vector of a thousand floats becomes a token of a dozen bits.
<ol>
<li>This may allow us to estimate an upper limit on the optimization power of an LLM outputting  tokens?</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Continuous chains of "thought" would be quite bad, since they'd increase the serial depth without information bottlenecks by a lot.</li>
<li>It now matters that all the circuits are aligned even when composed with each other, which is not guaranteed at all, and even having to extend the guarantees for alignment from every circuit to every ordered pair of circuits increases the size of the search space quadratically.
<ol>
<li>Though, I'm still not convinced this would give us ruthless optimizers. You gotta chain together <em>a lot</em> of short circuits in a semi-related fashion to result in strong optimization pressure/Goodharting/edge instantiation.</li>
</ol>
</li>
</ol>
<p>Most of the other things (backtracking in a forward pass is really hard
&amp;c) I'd otherwise say here have already been said by others.</p>
<p><strong>Training process</strong>: If we see this whole model as being about amortized
optimization, maybe it's the training process that takes up all the
optimization power? Are LLMs the most dangerous during training, or is
it rather the whole training process which is dangerous?</p>
<hr />
<p>I think this model is mostly correct, and also has implications
for capabilities progress/the need to switch to another paradigm/overhaul
parts of the current paradigm to reach wildly superhuman capabilities. I
think it predicts we'll see some gains from training but that we'll
plateau, or trade hard to measure capabilities for easily measurable
capabilities. I think I want to point to 55% "LLMs are agents" and 45%
"LLMs are stochastic parrots", but there's tons of AI capabilities
forecast questions I'm not yet able to answer (e.g. the infamous "which
concrete capability would you expect an LLM-based system not to have in
$YEAR?"). And plausibly the whole thing is just moot because long chains
of thought just enable enough chaining together to get the capabilities.</p>
<p>or smth idk</p>
<p>(Thanks to Claude 4.5 Sonnet for help and feedback. No part of the text was written by AI models.)</p>

<li><a href="https://generative.ink/posts/simulators/">Simulators (janus, 2022)</a></li>
</ol>

</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-4ZKjyFojJHza244qn-2"><p>The details of -sparsity are beyond the purview of this short note. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-4ZKjyFojJHza244qn-2">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-4ZKjyFojJHza244qn-3"><p><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.05451">Hänni et al. 2024</a> prove polynomial scaling, the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johnson-Lindenstrauss_lemma">Johnson-Lindenstrauss lemma</a>, taken too seriously, could imply exponential scaling. Polynomial scaling makes this picture more more feasible but less likely, since it's not clear a merely-polynomial number of circuits can deal with the complexity of the world with its exponentially growing observation-action sequences. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-4ZKjyFojJHza244qn-3">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
</ol>

### [Medical Roundup #7](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ypnYfPmn6FqAyxCpJ/medical-roundup-7)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>Things are relatively quiet on the AI front, so I figured it’s time to check in on some other things that have been going on, including various developments at the FDA.</p>


<h4>Table of Contents</h4>


<ol>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/190949631/fda-reformandum-est">FDA Reformandum Est.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/190949631/fda-delenda-est">FDA Delenda Est.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/190949631/in-mice">IN MICE.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/190949631/doctor-doctor">Doctor, Doctor.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/190949631/trust-the-process">Trust The Process.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/190949631/cancer-screening">Cancer Screening.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/190949631/autism-everywhere-all-at-once">Autism Everywhere All At Once.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/190949631/other-mental-problems-everywhere-all-at-once">Other Mental Problems Everywhere All At Once.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/190949631/source-data-verification">Source Data Verification.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/190949631/external-review-board">External Review Board.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/190949631/walk-it-off">Walk It Off.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/190949631/an-unhealthy-weight-can-be-worse-than-you-realize">An Unhealthy Weight Can Be Worse Than You Realize.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/190949631/our-glp-1-price-cheap">Our GLP-1 Price Cheap.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/190949631/right-to-die-should-include-right-to-try">Right To Die Should Include Right To Try.</a></li>
</ol>


<h4>FDA Reformandum Est</h4>


<p>In lieu of plan A, how about plan B?</p>
<p>Senator Bill Cassidy <a href="https://punchbowl.news/fda-modernization-whitepaperwdj2-16-26/">released a new report</a> on modernizing the FDA. <a href="https://marginalrevolution.com/marginalrevolution/2026/02/the-cassidy-report-on-the-fda.html?utm_source=rss&amp;utm_medium=rss&amp;utm_campaign=the-cassidy-report-on-the-fda">Alex Tabarrok approves</a>, which means it’s probably good.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25160"></span>


</div>
<p>The FDA chief has an even better idea.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/matthewherper/status/2024182272866255276">Matthew Herper</a>: FDA chief Marty Makary says ‘everything should be over the counter’ unless drug is unsafe or addictive [or requires monitoring].</p>
<p><a href="https://www.cnbc.com/2026/02/18/fda-chief-marty-makary-more-drugs-should-be-over-the-counter.html?__source=sharebar%7Ctwitter&amp;par=sharebar">Annika Kim Constantino</a>: Makary said the FDA is looking at “basic, safe” prescription drugs like nausea medications and vaginal estrogen, which is used to treat menopausal symptoms like dryness and pain.</p></blockquote>
<p>You’d also want a carveout for antibiotics, but yes. Your offer is acceptable, LFG.</p>
<p>That was the good news. There’s also the bad news, which was the main event.</p>


<h4>FDA Delenda Est</h4>


<p>Settle in for the tale of Moderna, and its attempt to get approval for a flu vaccine.</p>
<p>They were told their study design was acceptable. Then they got rug pulled. Then a lot of people were very loud and the FDA backtracked a bit while pretending not to.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/JeremiahDJohns/status/2022122216926982496">Jeremiah Johnson</a>: I remember reading that the EA case for Trump was that we’d see a flood of new drugs with a more innovation-friendly FDA.</p>
<p><a href="https://marginalrevolution.com/marginalrevolution/2026/02/i-regret-to-inform-you-that-the-fda-is-still-fdaing.html">Alex Tabarrok</a>: <strong>I Regret to Inform You that the FDA is FDAing Again.</strong></p>
<p>I had high hopes and low expectations that the FDA under the new administration would be less paternalistic and more open to medical freedom. Instead, what we are getting is paternalism with different preferences. In particular, the FDA now appears to have a bizarre anti-vaccine fixation, particularly of the mRNA variety (disappointing but not surprising given the leadership of RFK Jr.).</p></blockquote>
<p>It could have been one way. It’s the other way.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/mattparlmer/status/2022403455676223981">We’ve also lost Jim O’Neill</a>, who was basically our last best advocate for reasonable policy, but on the bright side <a href="https://x.com/gallabytes/status/2029323326766846314">he’s going to the National Science Foundation</a>. That’s also a good spot for him, but I worry his value over replacement will be a lower there.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/mattparlmer/status/2022403455676223981">mattparlmer</a>: I’ve been restraining myself from political commentary for the last few weeks, but I would be remiss not to register my extreme disappointment at the groundless sacking of Jim O’Neill</p>
<p>The Trump admin desperately needs competent people, Jim is exceptionally competent, this is bad</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/hamandcheese/status/2022520688041693627">Samuel Hammond</a>: welp</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ypnYfPmn6FqAyxCpJ/u0au4g7bf9oue6xfzhp4" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>
<p>This is what happens when you burn the term supervillain on people who want lower prices on drugs. What do you then call people who outright destroy one of the most promising medical advances of our time for no benefit whatsoever, via torching a $750 million study for decisions the FDA had explicitly already approved.</p>
<p>In this case, via withholding review for a drug that they had no business requiring the study for in the first place, in order to retroactively demand an absurd standard?</p>
<p>The FDA claims no, they didn’t say this study design was valid, but they’re lying, of course they did, there’s no way you spend $750 million dollars here without checking.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/cnnbrk/status/2021362479645732988">CNN Breaking News</a>: The FDA has refused to accept an application from Moderna to review its first mRNA seasonal flu vaccine, the company said.</p>
<p>The agency told Moderna that its application didn’t contain an “adequate and well-controlled” trial because the control arm didn’t reflect the “best-available standard of care in the United States at the time of the study,” according to <a href="https://static.modernatx.com/pm/6cef78f8-8dad-4fc9-83d5-d2fbb7cff867/38ab7558-636c-40d1-b49a-c8f51a7afa1a/38ab7558-636c-40d1-b49a-c8f51a7afa1a_viewable_rendition__v.pdf"><strong>a letter dated February 3</strong></a> that Moderna posted online. It didn’t identify any safety or efficacy concerns, the company said.</p>
<p>Moderna said that the refusal was inconsistent with previous feedback from the agency and that it had requested a meeting with FDA officials to understand how to proceed.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ATabarrok/status/2022375611860213908">Alex Tabarrok</a>: Hilarious: Trump admin is torn between those who say Moderna’s flu trial was unethical because it didn’t use a high-dose vaccine as comparator, exposing participants to “increased risk of severe illness,” and those who say it was unethical because it didn’t use an inert placebo!</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://www.statnews.com/2026/02/11/moderna-flu-vaccine-application-rejected-by-prasad-overruling-fda-staff/">Per STAT,</a> Prasad overruled FDA scientists to reject the application outright, and we have confirmation that staff was ready to review.</p>
<p>As an obvious technical note, we already have extensive data on how different existing vaccines relate to each other, so even if you thought that we shouldn’t allow a new offering until it was proven strictly superior and you changed your mind about the current standard, it is not statistically difficult to back out the necessary information.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/By_CJewett/status/2021361214912331914">Christina Jewett</a>: <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/02/10/health/fda-moderna-mrna-flu-vaccine.html?unlocked_article_code=1.LFA.RPma.OynIOUz9hU-D&amp;smid=url-share">NEW: The F.D.A. refused to accept an application from Moderna for its mRNA flu vaccine</a>.</p>
<p>Its reason: The agency did not think Moderna compared the new vaccine to one of the best flu shots available. The company spent $750M+ on a 41,000 person study.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/RuxandraTeslo/status/2021577069528768624">Ruxandra Teslo</a>: This is a very bad precedent. Regulatory agencies changing their stance after ok ing a study is one of the worst things for innovation.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/sdamico/status/2021459765688426620">Sam D’Amico</a>: This is going to waste a week of my time every other year for the next 20 years</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/LeahLibresco/status/2021950499734589758">Leah Libresco Sargeant</a>: “What is most disturbing is not the specifics of this case but the arbitrariness and capriciousness of the process. The EU, Canada, and Australia have all accepted Moderna’s application for review.”</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/By_CJewett/status/2021695062250312129">Christina Jewett</a> (NYT, February 11): UPDATE — FDA defends this refusal, but also retreats, calling this routine dialog with drug makers and saying it will work with Moderna —</p>
<p>The Food and Drug Administration on Wednesday backtracked on its refusal to review Moderna’s mRNA flu vaccine, while making an unusual public defense of its decisions on drug approvals.</p>
<p>… Dr. Marty Makary, the F.D.A. commissioner, suggested during a Fox News interview on Wednesday that the agency might eventually approve the vaccine. He characterized the rejection of Moderna’s application as “part of a conversation that occurs with all drug makers” and said the agency would continue to work with the company.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/matthewherper/status/2022142316232777951">Matthew Herper</a>: Here the Moderna flu data the FDA refused to review.</p>
<p>This is the most important slide from the company’s study of its experimental flu vaccine– exactly the data FDA said it wouldn’t even review.</p>
<p>And below is a thread of relevant slides from that presentation and links to other relevant studies I wanted to bookmark. (I’m a reporter who covers this stuff.)</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/matthewherper/status/2022142318808035833">Matthew Herper</a>: As you can see, volunteers who received Moderna’s flu shot, mRNA-1010, had about 26% less influenza-like illness in a 40,000 patient study.</p>
<p>These slides were presented to Moderna investors in November. Vinay Prasad, a top FDA official, told the company the vaccine would not be reviewed because the company compared to a standard, not high-dose, flu vaccine, and high-dose vaccines are standard for people over 65.</p>
<p>Here’s more background on the study. It enrolled, 40,000 people. They’re about evenly split between the 50-64 age group and the 65+ group.</p>
<p>mRNA vaccines can cause fatigue, achiness, and headache. They certainly saw that here.</p>

</blockquote>
<p><a href="https://investors.modernatx.com/events-presentations">The full slides are available here.</a> It is not obvious this particular vaccine is a good deal for most people, but it clearly works, and they did what the FDA said to do. The FDA pulled the plug anyway, not even examining the findings.</p>
<p>It is hard to overstate how destructive this is. This meaningfully hurts your quality of life, the number of days per year you can expect to be sick, and your long term life expectancy.</p>
<p>That’s because if Moderna has to worry about the FDA retroactively withdrawing its word on what studies will be accepted, after the work is done, and they are facing an mRNA-hostile FDA in general, they have no choice. They were already abandoning much of the work.</p>
<p>And everyone else has to have the same worry everywhere else. Oh no. At minimum, this forces ballooning costs as studies have to be designed defensively in case the wrong person at the FDA suddenly changes their mind.</p>
<p>Good luck implementing risk-based monitoring (RBM) or otherwise cutting costs, and many potential drugs will be abandoned.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/unusual_whales/status/2022359362862240203">unusual_whales</a>: Moderna’s, $MRNA, CEO has said that the company will no longer invest in new Phase 3 vaccine trials for infectious diseases: ‘You cannot make a return on investment if you don’t have access to the U.S. market.’ Vaccines for Epstein-Barr virus, herpes, and shingles have been shelved.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/drlsmittal/status/2022360039059821010">Dr. Lakshay Mittal</a>: When late-stage programs for Epstein–Barr, herpes and shingles get shelved, it shifts the pipeline risk profile of the entire biotech sector.</p>
<p>Investors will start asking which other infectious disease programs depend heavily on US market access.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/nickmmark/status/2021466904775491927">Nick Mark MD</a>: Three points:<br />
Moderna and FDA had previously agreed on the control arm dose. FDA went back on that agreement.</p>
<p>Presumably Prasad signed it because everyone else refused to.</p>
<p>If FDA pulls a switch like this it really kills biotechs incentive to do big trials. (this was n=40k)</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/DrPaulOffit/status/2023732242644697327">Paul Offit</a>: <a href="https://open.substack.com/pub/pauloffit/p/weaponizing-the-fda-against-vaccines?utm_campaign=post-expanded-share&amp;utm_medium=web">A closer look at the FDA’s recent kill shot against an mRNA influenza vaccine</a>.</p></blockquote>
<p>The pivot by Moderna to not invest in new Phase 3 trials was announced at Davos in January in the face of other symptoms of FDA hostility. Every person who gets Epstein-Barr, herpes or shingles more than a few years from now should know exactly who to blame for this outcome. That is only the start. Other companies, even those not focused on mRNA, have to now factor in this risk.</p>
<p>The good news is that we managed to yell loudly enough about this one that they may refuse course.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/By_CJewett/status/2021689779364991487">Christina Jewett</a>: Two points here. First, one of these two people is lying, and it would be a hell of a thing if the liar was Moderna. Second, whether people were previously from some point of view exposed to risk of illness has zero bearing on whether you should now expose everyone to risk of illness.</p>
<p>Saying in hindsight that the trial you approved was ‘unethical’ when it was clearly net beneficial to patients and there’s no case this contaminated the study? Complete and utter bullshit.</p></blockquote>
<p>Especially when you’re simultaneously arguing the exact opposite:</p>
<p>Mr. Kennedy long raised concerns as a vaccine activist that the inactive ingredients in vaccines could cause harm and should not be used in human trials.​</p>
<p>They say Kennedy was not involved here, but he’s head of the agency and has been furiously acting to undercut Moderna and its mRNA research at every turn.</p>
<p>One can try to frame this all as business as usual, and there are advantages to everyone coming away thinking about it that way whether or not it’s true:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/RxRegA/status/2021707062200480035">Jessica Adams</a>: The bad news is that I do not believe this was remotely normal. A lot of the damage is done and cannot be undone. The procedural decision itself is bad enough even if it is ultimately reversed. <a href="https://x.com/RxRegA/status/2024112778231869504">Mock the ‘chilling effect’</a> at your peril.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/venkmurthy/status/2024106748655685711/history">Venk Murthy tries to frame this</a> as ‘rebuilding trust’ by the public, but I expect this only further inflames popular paranoia about vaccines, because of the reversal, and the key ‘rebuilding trust’ that is suddenly necessary is the trust in the FDA by those researching vaccines. Without that, no new vaccines.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ATabarrok/status/2024553774119899419">Alex Tabarrok shares this</a>, which summarizes the ‘medical freedom’ movement driving much of this. Do you really think you can ‘rebuild trust’ with such folks?</p>

<p>It is important, when people’s objections are Obvious Nonsense, to point this out.</p>


<h4>IN MICE</h4>


<p>Progress is being made.</p>
<p>We now have a <a href="https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cx2g8rz7yedo">single vaccine could protect against all coughs, cold and flus, researchers say</a>. Well, okay, <a href="https://jamesheathers.medium.com/in-mice-explained-77b61b598218">IN MICE</a>. But it’s a start. The problem is, do they have the regulatory confidence to fully invest in that?</p>
<p>Stanford researchers identified 16-PGDH as a root cause of cartilage loss, claiming the mechanism works <a href="https://jamesheathers.medium.com/in-mice-explained-77b61b598218">IN MICE</a>, and also in human tissue samples. Epirium for now is doing Phase 1 trials for sarcopenia. FDA Delenda Est so we’re looking even in an optimistic case at 5-7 year timelines, but if it all works it’s possible this solves not only arthritis but a wide range of quality of life issues. Alas, this won’t do that much for life expectancy on its own since these mostly aren’t the things that kill you, but it could be a big win in QALY terms if it works out.</p>


<h4>Doctor, Doctor</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/carl_feynman/status/2016998220220563554">How should you describe your situation to a doctor</a>? You want to be respectful and honest with them, but if you dump a bunch of information they can get easily distracted in predictable ways. I buy Carl Feynman’s advice here to start with your diagnosis and key facts that seem most relevant and unlikely to mislead, then only later give the rest. Think of it as (polite) prompt engineering. LLMs can help you.</p>


<h4>Trust The Process</h4>


<p>This is one way to do plastic surgery, but you’d better be damn sure you’ve solved the associated principal-agent problems and the surgeon has good taste:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Aella_Girl/status/2014671489547776113">Aella</a>: Y’all my plastic surgery made me look moderately cuter. The feeling of seeing yourself more attractive in the mirror for the first time after the swelling finally fades is transcendent. You’ll have to pry the scalpel from my cold dead hands.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Aella_Girl/status/2014875912857371117">Aella</a>: my strategy this time was to go into the surgeon’s office, refuse to tell him my own concerns, ask “what would you do to make me look hotter”, and then blanket accept everything he suggested – even if I hadn’t thought I needed it.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/krishnanrohit/status/2014933788267249739">rohit</a>: –dangerously-skip-permissions</p></blockquote>
<p>It seems right that most people are not good judges of what would or would not make them look hotter, and that experts will know better, but also those experts have incentives. Perhaps you should focus more on establishing trust than trying to solve the problem yourself.</p>
<p>Also the person who most has to look at you will be you, so your preferences matter. Don’t fully ignore them. And yes, there is cumulative risk of backfire if you overdo it.</p>
<p>&nbsp;</p>


<h4>Cancer Screening</h4>


<p>AI but not LLMs: <a href="https://www.thelancet.com/journals/lancet/article/PIIS0140-6736(25)02464-X/abstract?utm_campaign=tlpr&amp;utm_medium=email&amp;_hsenc=p2ANqtz-9weNytsS31yFV1aopBRsT1ZJaRzXP-L6MgJeQPoaZXOFbeuS30DBAmTR5ZQg8F4joUBkkD5sld4qWIDAmOjiZ4E2av-g&amp;_hsmi=400595687&amp;utm_content=400595687&amp;utm_source=hs_email">New large study (n = 105,934) of AI in cancer screening</a> that started in April 2021 finds strong results from Transpara Version 1.7.0. Gains plausibly were largely about workflow integration and use of risk scores, rather than AI as a standalone second reader. Descriptively, there were overall 16% fewer invasive, 21% fewer large (T2+), and 27% fewer non-luminal A interval cancers in the intervention group compared with the control group, and the detection rate of cancer rose 29%, while enabling a 44% reduction in radiologist ‘screen readings.’ They got increased sensitivity without loss of specificity.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-01-29/ai-screening-linked-to-earlier-breast-cancer-detection-in-large-swedish-trial">I am deeply confused</a> by the whole ‘we still don’t know if this would be worth the cost’ response. What cost?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Mohamed_Foda/status/2017290160413569516">Mohamed Foda</a>: 100K women. 29% more cancers caught. 44% less workload. And somewhere a hospital administrator is still asking for a 3-year committee review before adopting AI. This is what happens when medicine actually evolves.</p></blockquote>
<p>That’s for old school AI that was available back in 2021. Diffusion not going great.</p>


<h4>Autism Everywhere All At Once</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.medpagetoday.com/neurology/autism/119747">Autism as it is now super broadly defined and aggressively diagnosed</a> may hit boys and girls in roughly equal measure. The marginal diagnosis clearly does net harm, and as explained previously there has been no actual increase in autism rates to explain.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/sapinker/status/2021281661045129240">Steven Pinker</a>: The autism epidemic is a myth: The increase is only in expansive overdiagnosis of kids “on the spectrum,” e.g. “Would rather be alone than with others,” “Has difficulty making friends,” and “Is regarded by other children as odd or weird.” <a href="https://t.co/mS6miGWM86">Severe cases (no language, socially unresponsive, etc.) have decreased</a>. By my former grad student @AdamOmaryPhD</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/JGBriggsPhD/status/2021335044737143167">Justin Gregory Briggs</a>: In the 20+ years I’ve been in the mental health field, the diagnostic criteria for autism have become so broad that they are clinically useless. Any spectrum that includes some of the most productive people on Earth and people who require 24/7 care is not interpretable.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Other Mental Problems Everywhere All At Once</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/Jesse_Leg/status/2013697377870180800">Jesse Arm also has a similar view</a> to this, as do I:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/ALegalProcess/status/2011784941756170550">TheLegalProcess (v3.0 | Instruction Not Therapy)</a>: Honored to be a contributor with @CarolynGorman_ on this issues brief and model legislation for @ManhattanInst . In 2025, IL and LA accelerated the use of universal mental health screenings in schools.</p>
<p><a href="https://manhattan.institute/article/universal-mental-health-screening-in-schools-a-critical-assessment">We take a contrarian view. Schools are not mental health clinics: ban them</a>.</p>
<p>Universal mental health screening is ineffective at creating prosocial behavior and improved academic outcomes. The practice is also harmful – and occurs almost wholly unregulated by state boards of behavioral health.</p>
<p>Carolyn D. Gorman: This issue brief provides an overview of universal school-based mental health screening, related law and evidence, and practical and ethical concerns. It finds that:</p>
<ol>
<li>Universal mental health screenings are ineffective: they do not reduce the prevalence of mental health conditions or improve academic outcomes.</li>
<li>Universal mental health screenings are harmful: they produce overwhelmingly high rates of false positives and, when delivered in schools, have fewer protections in place than in clinical settings.</li>
</ol>
<p>Given the lack of evidence for universal mental health screening, this brief recommends its prohibition in schools. For targeted and individual school-based mental health screenings, far stronger regulatory conditions are warranted. Model legislation is provided for implementing these recommendations.</p></blockquote>
<p>Mental health screenings sound like a good idea, but the false positives and overtreatment and medicalization of problems that result in practice mean they end up doing more harm than good. Such screenings look for trouble, and trouble they often find, which can be devastating for kids and families.</p>


<h4>Source Data Verification</h4>


<p><a href="https://learninghealthadam.substack.com/p/why-clinical-trials-are-inefficient">Adam explains some of the reasons clinical trials are inefficient.</a></p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://learninghealthadam.substack.com/p/why-clinical-trials-are-inefficient">Adam</a>: There are many examples of waste and inefficiency in clinical trials, but I want to highlight among these, one practice stands out: a practice called 100% source data verification, which is both distinctly wasteful and can tell us a lot about what does – and what doesn’t – drive the inefficiencies in today’s trials.</p>
<p>… After the site sends the data they collected to the sponsor, the sponsor sends a consultant to the site to pore through their paper records to make sure that every single data element the site collected matches the data the sponsor received.</p></blockquote>
<p>That seems rather obviously crazy. Even the FDA now agrees. Yet Adam claims this is what typically happens.</p>
<blockquote><p>100% source data verification is a huge task. The typical phase 3 clinical trial collects <a href="https://www.fiercebiotech.com/cro/one-third-data-collected-clinical-trials-may-be-unnecessary-study-finds">3 million data points</a>. So it is perhaps not surprising that it’s expensive to check them all: the process of 100% source data verification accounts <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11639101/">for 25%-40% of clinical trial costs</a>. <strong>And it’s completely pointless</strong>. It <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5718334/">doesn’t meaningfully help</a> with clinical trial quality. The FDA has <a href="https://www.fda.gov/regulatory-information/search-fda-guidance-documents/oversight-clinical-investigations-risk-based-approach-monitoring">publicly </a>and <a href="https://www.fda.gov/regulatory-information/search-fda-guidance-documents/risk-based-approach-monitoring-clinical-investigations-questions-and-answers">repeatedly </a>recommended against doing it for over ten years.​</p>
<p>Nobody has a very good explanation for why the industry continues this practice.</p></blockquote>
<p>Easy. Ban the practice, as in have the FDA require you not to do it. Alternatively, Claude thinks the concrete fear is how things will look on audits, so you can create rules to protect you from audits.</p>
<p>Instead we have the opposite, now more than ever. The FDA is announcing it will look for any excuse to deny your application in at least some cases.</p>
<p>The question then is, are there cases where the FDA will look for any excuse to say no, but end up having to say yes anyway? There might not be. A common pattern when seeking approval is that you beat yourself up over details that might matter, and then you get told one of your choices mattered, when really their mind was already made up for other reasons.</p>
<p>Also I saw claims that this<a href="https://x.com/PatientPersists/status/2020440201424753132"> particular data point does not hold up</a>. Claude notes SPV is not universal, but thinks the claim of 25%-40% of total costs is plausible.</p>
<p>The other big obvious problem is that our scientists have yet to learn <a href="https://www.civilopedia.net/rise-and-fall/technologies/tech_mass_production">the secret of mass production</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p>Unfortunately, today’s approach to clinical trials looks much more like NASA’s “Big Space” approach than SpaceX’s iterative approach. Each trial is created from scratch. Researchers develop a protocol custom-designed to study a specific drug. Then, infrastructure, researchers, and contractors are all onboarded for that specific trial.</p>
<p>Finally, that one-of-a-kind trial is executed, carrying its precious cargo: the pharmaceutical company’s treasured pharmaceutical asset, with billions of dollars of revenue on the line.​</p></blockquote>
<p>This again seems obviously crazy. There should be a standard product called a clinical trial, you should do it in standard ways over and over again.</p>
<p>Why no fixes?</p>
<blockquote><p>Could one of the incumbents in this industry break through this morass? It’s unlikely because <strong>there are no players in the industry with the ability, resources, and incentive to drive improvement</strong>.​</p>
<p>It starts with a lack of motivation. <strong>Pharmaceutical companies don’t face competitive pressure over trial costs</strong>.</p>
<p>… But let’s say a company were aware of these problems and genuinely wanted to do something about them. They still might lack the internal capacity to do so.</p>
<p>… The clinical trials industry has a culture problem. In short, they don’t sufficiently acknowledge that they are an <em>industry </em>in the first place.</p>
<p>… The result:<strong> leaders and policymakers don’t apply an engineering mindset to trials</strong>.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ypnYfPmn6FqAyxCpJ/czm5yhljhauq1lgxserb" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>
<p>I don’t buy the ‘no motivation’ argument, as reduced costs drive more profits, and there are at least several big players who do enough iterating to justify this, and if they figured it out they could turn the trials into a new profit center.</p>
<p>This feels like a clear ‘if he wanted to’ situation. You can fix the cultural issue if you care enough. The real problem is indeed cultural, and it’s that they consider everything involved sacred, and everyone demands the everything bagels at all times, and those are sufficient legal and other barriers that no one dares try to fix things.</p>
<p>&nbsp;</p>


<h4>External Review Board</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/RuxandraTeslo/status/2014385714654236866">Why not let researchers choose their own accredited IRB</a>, at the risk of taking some of the internal out of the review board? This is very much a ‘mostly disarm the IRBs’ proposal and I fully support it. That’s because I think IRBs are totally out of control and cause orders of magnitude more harm than they prevent, so letting people shop for the most efficient and accommodating one seems fine. The point would be that someone independent of you has to put themselves and their reputation on the line to say you’re doing something reasonable and not doing any blatant ethical violations, whereas right now no one can do much of anything.</p>


<h4>Walk It Off</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/EricTopol/status/2013761769764737239">A new large observational study claims</a> that 45 minutes a day of walking gives you most of the all-cause mortality benefits of exercise, and running doesn’t do better than walking until you do rather a lot of it, whereas too much jogging can actively backfire and swimming and bicycling do little. Doing multiple types of activity was better.</p>
<p>Note that the X-axis on different graphs can be very different here.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ypnYfPmn6FqAyxCpJ/b2mssjulhrlkmj1zisub" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>It’s an observational and self-reported study, so you can’t assume causation and doubtless ‘willing and able’ correlations should be doing a lot of work. Presumably people who swim or bicycle must have some real problems? But there’s still a lot of explaining to do and in some places that only raises further questions.</p>
<p>The obvious response is to defy the data, or at least some of it, or at least attribute it to selection effects. It doesn’t make any sense for bicycling and swimming to have about zero effect and jogging to start actively backfiring this much, while walking cuts mortality by 20%.</p>
<p>I do buy that this is evidence for ‘if you do a lot of walking that largely counts being active and doing exercise.’</p>


<h4>An Unhealthy Weight Can Be Worse Than You Realize</h4>


<p>Being in generally poor health causes all sorts of other issues, part quite a lot:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/estherzelda0514/status/2015853742546157811">Not a Good Jewish Girl</a>: My fiancée is extremely mad that losing 20 pounds with a GLP-1 has basically cured her sleep and back issues and made her PMS symptoms much more tolerable, improving her overall mood on a daily basis and making her job less stressful.</p>
<p>These drugs should be fully subsidized.</p></blockquote>
<p>The obvious way to do that is to buy one of the drugs and give it away, or sell it as a generic at marginal cost. It’s fine to pay the NPV of future royalties to get it, it’s a win-win, and you only have to buy one of them so you can play them off against each other.</p>


<h4>Our GLP-1 Price Cheap</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/ArthurB/status/2019727850870300783">Arthur B.</a>: The US government could buy the patent for some oral GLP agonist like orforglipron for under $30B, give it away with Medicaid and save about $200B a year in Medicaid / Medicare future spending on obesity-linked diseases.</p></blockquote>
<p>Or the market can solve this on its own, if this crosses the threshold and becomes worthwhile enough that consumers pay cash. Go go gadget market incentives.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/health/pharma/the-weight-loss-price-wars-are-breaking-big-pharmas-business-model-53446a58">David Wainer</a>: Two years ago, a GLP-1 prescription could cost an uninsured patient more than $1,000 a month. Today, Novo Nordisk’s Wegovy pill starts at just $149 through cash-pay programs.</p>
<p>… Then on Thursday, shares of both companies fell sharply after <a href="https://www.wsj.com/market-data/quotes/HIMS">Hims &amp; Hers Health</a> announced a $49-a-month compounded version of the Wegovy pill. They rebounded Friday after the Food and Drug Administration chief threatened action against the mass marketing of copycat drugs.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/scottlincicome/status/2020135722808737901">Scott Lincicome</a>: Fascinating: Eager GLP-1 consumers bypassed the traditional employer-based insurance system and instead turned to cash/direct-payments (+ telehealth scrips), <a href="https://wsj.com/health/pharma/the-weight-loss-price-wars-are-breaking-big-pharmas-business-model-53446a58?mod=panda_wsj_custom_topic_alert">leading to a Big Pharma price war and now cheap brand-name drugs</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ingelramdecoucy/status/2020164066178646238/history">Enguerrand VII de Coucy</a>: I find it difficult to understand how this could have happened, it’s almost like a force that we can’t see intervened in the GLP-1 market to bring prices down as unrestrained market competition increases… an invisible foot stomped down prices, perhaps? Translucent elbow?</p></blockquote>
<p>Then again, <a href="https://www.wsj.com/health/pharma/novo-nordisk-escalates-fight-against-hims-hers-69c15e2b?mod=WTRN_pos1">perhaps the market is solving this via patent infringement</a>? Novo Nordisk says so in their lawsuit against Hims &amp; Hers.</p>


<h4>Right To Die Should Include Right To Try</h4>


<p>In Canada and want to try an experimental treatment? <a href="https://x.com/RuxandraTeslo/status/2026982271782101489">We can’t allow that, would you instead like to have us help you commit suicide</a>?</p>
<p>I think it’s reasonable to be for or against assisted suicide to varying degrees. But if someone would be allowed to invoke MAID and kill themselves, then you have to also allow them (on their own dime as needed) to then do any other crazy thing that only might kill them. <a href="https://marginalrevolution.com/marginalrevolution/2026/02/if-you-have-the-right-to-die-you-should-have-the-right-to-try.html">As Alex Tabarrok puts it: If you have the right to die, you should have the right to try</a>, at least if you absolve the system of your future oversized directly related medical expenses.</p>
<p>Meanwhile:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/leah_pierson/status/2029022572654760301">Leah Pierson</a>: omg this title, <a href="https://jme.bmj.com/content/47/12/e90">this paper</a></p>
